ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u17	I'm asking to talk.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u18	You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u19	Gasparri (FI-PdL XVII). Mr President, I have not spoken so far in the broad debate we have had on the various articles, but at this point a speech on the amendments that I have tabled together with other colleagues in my Group is essential. We are well aware that there is no broad convergence in this House on the subject of the direct popular election of the President of the Republic; however, the Forza Italia Group makes this proposal one of the peculiarities of its overall vision of the reform of the institutions. I would like to remind you that the theme of direct democracy, of the popular election of the executive leadership, has had various forms of representation. For example, in the constitutional reform that was approved in 2006, and then not confirmed by the referendum, the direct election was about the Premier, and I would also like to remind you that if that reform had come into force we would have had a Parliament reduced in numbers since 2008, a bipolarism consolidated with the direct election of the Premier and certainly also an electoral law that would follow consequently a system of election of the Premier with a ballot. This has not been the case, and therefore the delay that the institutions and politics are experiencing is also the result of the development of those events. Moreover, the Constitution provides for a confirmation referendum; those who have promoted it have acted within the prerogatives provided for in the Constitution and today we find ourselves, eight years late, discussing these matters. There have been moments of convergence, although the theme of the direct election of the Premier or the Head of State has not been part of the subjects that have recorded a broad and transversal sharing. I would also like to mention, Mr President, the essential contribution to this reform process of the Forza Italia Group. Since the President of the Council has repeatedly praised the Senators of the majority who support the Government, let me also claim the merits of our Group without whose contribution we probably would not have reached a decisive and turning point in this reform. We have supported this reform, although it does not represent our objectives 100%. A few weeks ago, President Berlusconi, explaining the positions of Forza Italia, reiterated our adherence to the reform because he is pursuing the overcoming of bicameralism, the reduction of the number of parliamentarians, however the improvement of the decision-making mechanisms. Therefore, for these reasons, we share a reform, albeit partial to our objectives, and it was precisely President Berlusconi who reminded us of the amendments on presidentialism that we had tabled. These amendments, I would like to stress this because they are not a sine qua non for us: partial and insufficient reform, however, we will agree with them, because, as I said just now, it will help to make the institutional mechanisms more efficient, but we believe that the House should still speak on the issue of the direct election of the Head of State. You have reserved a decision on whether or not the amendments are admissible, someone has also objected to the procedures, but I want to acknowledge that in addition to the essential contribution of the Forza Italia Group, without which we would not have reached this point, even the decisions you have taken in various sensitive passages have allowed the reform to reach the point where we are, so if you considered taking the decision after the illustrations you would have had your good reason to do so. I believe that the dilemma is solved very simply by Article 97 (1) of the Senate's Rules of Procedure, which I invite all, including the members of the Government, who are new to events of this kind, to read, because it clearly indicates that there must be a homogeneity of matter, and the homogeneity is certainly there. In the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, this issue has already been raised and I have had a lot of comparisons with the President, rapporteur, Mrs Finocchiaro, in the case of the work of the Commission, and also with the exchanges of letters. President Finocchiaro did not consider that he could accept in the Commission the amendments which could affect changes to the governing structures, considering that the reform process should be confined to certain parts of the functioning of Parliament, Title V of Part II and other matters that are at the heart of this proposal. I have expressed a different view, and now this issue of admissibility is again with another interlocutor, who is the President of this House. In my view, Rule 97 of the Rules of Procedure makes this issue incontrovertible. I have, however, tabled a number of amendments which I have multiplied in view of the House's examination. The amendments tabled at first alter in some way the structure of government and give the President of the Republic other powers: there is an amendment, for example, that on the French model allows the President of the Republic to preside over the Council of Ministers, even in the presence of a Prime Minister. I do not dwell on the matter, not least because of a question of the economy of time, but in France it works like this: there is the Prime Minister, but the President elected by the people is the depositary of powers, responsibility and he can also participate in the meetings of the Government, which, of course, presides over his office. Moreover, Mr Grasso, the amendments I tabled were baptized by the House in this very Chamber - it will be remembered by the non-numerous veterans of the previous legislature - where I and Senator Quagliariello signed during the debate on the reform of the Constitution. Those amendments were adopted, I proposed them again in the same text. That reform did not eventually follow, so attempts remained. However, even by accepting the objection made by Senator Finocchiaro in the first Commission, according to which we cannot substantially affect the form of government and therefore the powers of the Head of State, I have tabled two other amendments - which, in particular, Mr Grasso - concerning changes which, in my opinion, do not affect, at least in a formal way, the function of the President of the Republic, except for the fact, my friends, that the substantial function of the Head of State has instead been transformed. As you know, we have a material constitution that is different from the formal one. For many years now the role of the President of the Republic has been transformed: I remember the presidency Scalfaro, the events of the failure to break up Parliament in 1994, the Dini Government, then the Ciampi Presidency and the Napolitano Presidency, (the latter, among other things, repeated by a large majority by the will of Parliament), the Monti Government, a whole series of events that have made the role of the President of the Republic much more incisive. Moreover, the crisis of the political system itself and the development of events have made it possible for the Presidency of the Republic - and I just note this - to take on a different role than in the past. I added then, President Grasso, two other amendments that, leaving the form of government as well as the 1948 Constitution draws it, provide for the direct election by citizens of a President of the Republic with the same powers as the Constitution gives him today: that then the real ones have grown in time is a fact of reality, as politologists and scholars would say, and that certainly does not depend on our debate. In addition to the amendment for the popular election of the President of the Republic without any change of powers - thus remaining the Government, the confidence, the appointment of the Prime Minister and everything that the Constitution provides and which we know well - I have foreseen an amendment, shared by my Group and presented by other colleagues - I see here in the Chamber President Casini, who explained this position - which leaves Parliament, in the first instance, the duty and the right to elect the Head of State. If, after the three votes involving a two-thirds quorum, Parliament fails to express itself, at that point, instead of proceeding with further votes with reduced quorums - there are amendments by other Members who have included amendments in this regard too - in the absence of a positive outcome, then we move on to the people, that is to say to the direct election. I shall begin by concluding, because there could be a variety of considerations on presidentialism, which I refer to possible explanations of vote, which I hope will be followed by a positive opinion on the admissibility of the amendments that we are talking about. I believe - and I say this by preventing the speeches of some of my colleagues, including Senator Zanda - that the issue is largely admissible. On the election of the Head of State, the Commission has already intervened, in some way changing the composition of the electoral body: there are amendments by other Members proposing the involvement of MEPs or the modification of quorums that can be determined as soon as possible. I therefore leave out the arguments in favour of presidentialism, direct democracy and the stability of the institutions, which would be strengthened by this choice. On the other hand, I have no doubt about eligibility, at least of the amendments which - subject to the current forms of government - provide for direct, immediate or subsequent elections to three non-bearing votes in Parliament, because I believe that they must be discussed. Let us assume a responsibility: Forza Italia does not put an alternative between doing this or not doing the reform; we will go on anyway, but we cannot ask ourselves to give up a political instance to which, ladies and gentlemen, the country will arrive. After the election of the mayors and presidents of the Regions, in fact, electing a decision-maker at national level is the need for a modern democracy from which we will not be able to escape, and sooner or later we will get there. [[Applause from the FI-PdL Group XVII.]] I think it is right, therefore, to discuss this in the context of this reform, Mr President: the government will make its own assessments, it could also go back to the House. However, we will go on to conclude this reform, but let us allow ourselves to include in this debate a matter of great importance and great modernity. [[Applause from the FI-PdL Group XVII.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u20	I'm asking to talk.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u21	You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u22	MARAN (SPI). Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I, together with another group of colleagues, have a strong preference for the French semi-presidency system. For this reason, I presented a bill to introduce into our legal system the French equal system: this is the Senate Act No. 250, communicated to the Senate Presidency on 21 March 2013. My draft law, however, was excluded from the discussion in the Commission, because the question of the form of government was judged to be unrelated to the ongoing discussion. I shall now explain very briefly why I think it would be preferable to introduce the French system into our system. Its rules and institutions make a significant contribution to the restructuring of parties and their competition. In France, the restructuring of the parties had as they fly the competition for the Presidency of the Republic. This is a system that would be preferable, because presidential politics is now an integral part of the national scene: since 1993 we have elected directly the Government of Municipalities, Provinces and Regions; a few months ago - as stated in President Letta's programme - we had the ambition to elect the President of the European Union directly in the future. We do not understand why the only level that we cannot directly elect is the national level. The French system is also preferable because metamorphosis has already occurred: there are no longer identification and membership, in the ideology in the morale of parties, which can only be replaced by the exercise of the citizenship of the State, with the choice of citizens. It is from the referendums of 1991-1993 that the Italians answered clearly the question that we had asked them: Is it the parties or the citizens who choose the government? And that answers to the parties or the citizens? Since then, we have used electoral laws to answer that question improperly. I would also add that a luminary like Professor Sartori made it clear that the construction of a premiership system on the Westminster model or as in Germany or Spain largely escapes the claims of constitutional engineering, in the sense that it works in that way because there are favourable circumstances, like the great parties, that we no longer have. A non-progressive passage, like shock therapy, would be preferable, according to a scientist like Professor Sartori. Having said all this, however, I believe that the debate we are holding is frankly unacceptable: it is not possible that my draft law has not been discussed nor has it been possible to address the question of the form of government, because it is excluded, whereas today, surreptitiously, with some amendments, it is thought to introduce an issue that should completely redo the architecture of the State. As far as I am concerned, the matter is quickly resolved: either my proposal for a law should have been discussed or the amendments could not be discussed. [[Applause from the PD Group and Senator Ichino.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u23	I'm asking to talk.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u24	You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u25	CASINI (PI). Mr President, it is clear that my amendment 21.4 has a different scope, because the direct election that I have proposed takes place in a very narrow area from the point of view of the institutional situation: it is intended to be a deterrent to a quarrelsome Parliament which does not reach the quorums envisaged, i.e. 51% higher, in order to elect the Head of State. My proposal is aimed at preventing the Head of State from being imposed by one of the parties. The Head of State is safeguarded in his role as a third party: if the prescribed quorums are not reached in the votes, the election is carried out by universal suffrage and direct by ballot between the first two votes of Parliament, and this also protects the institutions from the risk of external populist adventures, because the two most voted personalities in Parliament contribute, with a sort of supplement of the electorate, to a competition limited to them. I realize that this is a very important matter. Mr Tosato proposed it to the constituent Assembly, the illustrious colleague, Mr Maccanico, proposed it again in a draft law a few years ago. I realize that this proposal risks introducing a big issue like a house, that is, the issue of the direct election of the President of the Republic, albeit in a different logic, as I have said; but I believe that we must find a system and a mechanism to safeguard the third-party status of the Head of State, because with this reform that we are implementing we risk having a Head of State who is the majority. The Government and Minister Boschi have understood this; somehow the Minister has also tried to meet my objection with the raising of the quorum and the widening of the votes for which quorum is required; but, in my opinion, it always ends there, with the possibility of electing the President of the Republic with a quorum of 51%. For these reasons, I have proposed this amendment 21.4 conscientiously to the House and I will vote for it, although I realise it and do not want to create difficulties for the Commission, but these are the terms of the bare and raw question: the need to have a mechanism to safeguard the third-party status of the Head of State. [[Applause from Senator Rossi Maurizio.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u29	I'm asking to talk.

ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u31	Mr Quegliariello (NCD). Mr President, this debate highlights a paradox of reform with regard to the form of government: those who have historically supported the presidential or semi-presidency form have in the past been accused of wanting to promote a "plebiscytic derivative" in this country - that was the most popular expression - of giving in to the temptation to give too much power to the direct choice of citizens. In a situation in which the bicameral system fails, and at a time when the audience of the great voters for the Presidency of the Republic narrows, with a House - the political one - governed by a large majority prize, the paradox is evident: a direct choice by the citizens can become a guarantee and almost a rule of closing the system, rather than lend itself to the risks that had been feared. We are witnessing, that is, some sort of paradigm reversal. This is, therefore, the reason why we believe that the form of government should be discussed within this legislature. I would also add that it is also the reason why my party has even promoted a law of popular initiative in this regard, so that this indication can also come from the citizens and be strengthened. Finally, Mr President, once again let me stress that this is also the reason why we believed that constitutional reforms should be discussed in a parliamentary committee with the necessary scope, because changing a part of the Constitution necessarily implies changes on other parts. Having said that, we have noted that a different choice has been made and that on the contrary - I say it without controversy, but for the sake of historical truth - with the fundamental contribution of Forza Italia we have chosen another method, that is to achieve reforms piece by piece. This implies having a vision of the whole, extending and projecting one's action in the long run, but under no circumstances can it lead to introducing an issue such as the form of government - much larger than a house, Senator Casini - into a debate such as the one we are conducting through one amendment. Mr President, I therefore believe that we need to confirm what has already been decided in the Commission, partly because otherwise we would be faced with an asymmetrical geometry debate, so that some issues have not been discussed in the Commission but are debated directly in this House. This is not a choice against semi-presidentialism, against the fact that we are dealing with the form of government. On the contrary, I believe that dealing with it is absolutely necessary. It is only a serious choice so that we take good care of this issue, in our own place, and so that the effort we have begun to support can have an extension and the determinations that we are taking through the change of bicameralism can be considered on the other sensitive parts of our Constitutional Charter with the necessary deepening. That is why, Mr President, we would obviously be in favour of this debate having its own seat and not being held in a context which would be objectively inconsistent. [[Applause from the NCD and PD groups.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u32	I'm asking to talk.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u33	You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u34	De Cristofaro (Misto-SEL). Mr President, I rise to explain the amendments 21.10 and 21.14 tabled by the Mixed-SEL Group in order to broaden the audience that elects the Head of State. Over the last few days we have tried to explain the reasons for this, which I will repeat again in just a few minutes. First of all, there is an entirely political fact, but it risks having a very great weight on the constitutional reform that we are about to launch. I am referring to the separate debate for which we are dealing on the one hand with constitutional reform and on the other the issue of electoral law (which, moreover, continues, as we are seeing here, once again with separate meetings and not held within the parliamentary classrooms). The fact that we are dealing with these two reforms in parallel, but without crossing them, from our point of view, is likely to pose a huge problem with which we will face up in the years to come. This is one of the fundamental reasons for our radical criticism of the idea of reform that is being defined, which - in our opinion - does not work, is messy, even with regard to the election of the Head of State. Mr President, it is the Head of State, that is to say, the one who, as stated in Article 87 of the Constitution, represents national unity, and therefore is not really a secondary element. Within the constitutional architecture that is being defined, we imagine an electoral law, with which we will confront ourselves in the years to come, which introduces very significant innovations in the Italian order, i.e. barriers and prizes of higher majority than those we have known over the years. In short, there is a certain tendency, and I do not say so, but authoritative constitutionalists. Zagrebelsky himself, this morning, wrote of the tendency to reduce the Chambers to recorders of decisions of the Executive, even at the risk of constitutionalizing the subdiction of legislative power towards the executive power, that is, by introducing into the national order a mechanism whereby a party that simply has the relative majority of seats takes everything, transforms that relative majority into an absolute majority through the majority prizes and even elects the President of the Republic. You will understand well that in this case we are faced with a new fact with which - I have repeated it a thousand times in these days and now I insist for the umpteenth time - we should have faced ourselves with much more serious and in a less messy way, even on the issues just put that concern presidentialism and semi-presidentialism. Historically and still today we are opposed to the direct election of the Head of State, for many reasons that I will not repeat in this House because we have had the opportunity to explain them so many times over the last few years. Mr President, I invite you and of course the whole House to reflect on the fact that at least the presidential or semi-presidentialist system provides for a mechanism of weights and counterweights; for example, it does provide for direct election, but also for strict rules on a subject such as the conflict of interest (to name one). In our country, instead, we risk to carry out the debate a little surreal that leads to a latent presidentialism, which does not foresee the direct election of the Head of State (but which, I repeat, has at least counterweights), but a mechanism by which a party, a single political force, even with a simple relative majority, can dispose of the order as it pleases. Ladies and gentlemen, we believe that this issue is very serious and refers to a fundamental point, for which we will not vote in favour of constitutional reform (you will vote for it yourself). And do you know what the bottom line is? It is that a constitutional reform mechanism is being built that starts from a radically wrong assumption. This radically wrong assumption is the convenience of today: this is the point on which we disagree. We should have started with a basic assumption that we should have sculpted in this Chamber of the Senate and in the Chamber of Deputies when the measure comes to the other branch of Parliament: constitutional rules are made to apply over time; they are made to assert, not just tomorrow morning when, in fact, a given political condition will lead to a given electoral result, but they are made to apply for years, if not for decades. So, as we are discussing issues relating to the state's architecture for the coming decades, we should have imagined a mechanism for the protection of weights and counterweights and, let's say, much more democratic guarantees, but much more serious than what we are doing. From our point of view, this is a major concern. Let us hope that some of today's amendments - I am referring to those, like ours, which refer to the enlargement of the audience of those who elect the Head of State - will be adopted, because we think that this is useful to imagine, not simply a system of greater counterweights, but - also - a political system in which it is still possible for minorities to play a role that, of course, is not that of government (because of course it governs the majority and not the minority), but of control and guarantee. These are essential instruments on which, moreover, the constituent Fathers lingered long when they put their hand to the Italian Constitution, now many decades ago. I understand that the lesson of the constituent Fathers - unfortunately - has been somewhat forgotten during these years and then, let's say, withdrawn from the cylinder simply when we discussed immunity. However, that teaching should be kept in mind even today with great rigour. For many weeks now we have been warning that these points are raised, which concern not the authoritarian turning point, but the elements of the functioning of Italian democracy, the elements of the functioning and guarantee of political minorities and the elements which, in some way, make a democratic process complete. [[Applause from the Misso-SEL Group.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u35	I'm asking to talk.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u36	You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u37	Mr Gotor (PD). Mr President, I would like to explain amendment 21.8 which proposes to extend the college of major voters who will elect the President of the Republic. It consists of 730 members, including MEPs and new regional senators, and the amendment also proposes to extend it to 73 Italian Members of the European Parliament. As far as I am aware, the amendment has collected more than 90 signatures and has, therefore, a large and qualified institutional and parliamentary dimension; it is a valuable opportunity to enhance the role and autonomy of the Senate during this constitutional reform. Among the signatories there are about sixty colleagues of the Democratic Party; the former President of the Chamber, Senator Pier Ferdinando Casini, who generously agreed to bring together his similar amendment in this; two vice presidents of the Senate; all the group leaders of the majority government present in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs; for the opposition, representatives of the Mixed-Italy Group Work in progress. In addition, the amendment received the informal appreciation of so many Members of the European Parliament, who are supporting us at this time, and of many colleagues who, for reasons of understandable political opportunity, have decided not to sign it, but they share the spirit of this initiative. Why so many signatories of so different political forces? Perhaps because the amendment has the merit of addressing a problem, that of the guarantees and the overall balance of the system, for which there is a cross-sensibility between all the political groups, as emerged from the debate in the Commission over the past few months and also from many speeches in this House. Many have stressed the need for an organic approach to institutional reforms, aware that if we intervene on the balance of bicameralism and at the same time on electoral law, it is inevitable that the form of government will also undergo changes, albeit indirect, that we must know how to predict and evaluate in their consequences as a whole. The amendment is motivated by two reasons. The first: the signatories believe that the time has come - just as the Constitutional Council of 1946 identified the representation of future regional legislators as a significant part of the presidential election college - that European Parliamentarians should also be recognised as having the task of contributing, with their experience, to the choice of the President of the Republic, whose constitutional figure is increasingly involved in the evolution of the form of the State in a European sense. It is no coincidence that the most well-known doctrine has defined interparliamentary cooperation as the third life of the European Parliament, tracing in the relationship between it and the national parliaments of the Member States a genuine Euro-national parliamentary system. Nor are there any objections of constitutional consistency to this solution, nor would it be possible for the European Parliament or the European Union, since the institutions of the Union are committed by the Treaties to respect for the constitutional identity of the Member States. Secondly, the main objective of this amendment - and I would like to draw your attention here - is to mitigate the combined effect of a necessary majority rule for the election of the House of Deputies by reducing the number of 215 senators and the elimination of the body of the 58 regional delegates from the audience of the great voters. In fact, according to the text approved by the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, the winner of the majority political prize could elect the head of state in a self-sufficient way from the ninth ballot onward, i.e. after only four days of blank voting, by controlling the vote of only 26 regional senators. As you know, in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, an agreement had been reached between the rapporteurs and the Government, which envisaged maintaining the 58 major regional voters, that is, three per Region, which, however, jumped for the opposition of Forza Italia. It seems that Forza Italia would also be against this amendment on MEPs because the Democratic Party, according to the last elections, would have a larger number of MEPs. However, it should be borne in mind that if the next Head of State - as is most likely - is still elected with the old rules - probable and desirable - the new rules will enter into force in the next European legislature, after 2019, with balances that can be changed again. As you know, in the Commission, thanks also to the availability of Forza Italia and the sensitivity of the rapporteurs, the Democratic Party has succeeded in having an amendment adopted, of which I was the first signatory, which intervenes on the strengthening of quorums. But this solution is not enough, and it only means admitting that, after four days of voting that has been empty, from the ninth ballot, those who have won the majority prize will be able to elect the Head of State and the guarantee bodies that result in a self-sufficient way, with less than one third of the regional senators. The problem, which today seems abstract or mitigated by the fact that secret voting is in place in the elections of the Presidents of the Republic, could arise in its concreteness in the event of the convergence of two circumstances, which we have a duty to foresee at all. The first circumstance is when the general elections coincide with the end of the seven-year term of the President of the Republic (as was the case in 2006 and 2013). The second is in the event that a new force breaks into the system, winning the majority prize by a great career with an action of break-in that the current mobility of the political vote in Europe and some recent experiences at national and foreign level make a possibility anything but unlikely. This new force, precisely because it is new, could not at the same time have sufficient Members of the European Parliament and regional senators to elect the Head of State alone. In conclusion, Mr President, I would like to address my fellow Members. Let us be careful, because this Constitution assigns to the President of the Republic a role of referee and guarantor, perhaps as a reserve engine of the system in circumstances of crisis, but never as head of a political force or a political part. The wisdom of the constituent Fathers has given the Head of State powers to accordion, which can be opened or closed according to the circumstances and which, even in recent times, have proved to be a valuable asset. For these reasons I trust that the Government and the rapporteurs will be able to return to the Chamber and, as was the case with the question of immunity, a debate can be opened on the basis of the relevance of the matter. The Constitution must assume as a supreme guiding value that of institutional prudence and must exclude, even theoretically, solutions that would insert a plebiscytic twist in our system: not authoritarian, but plebiscytic. The concepts must be kept completely separate because they are different, but able to change the balance and quality of democracy in our country. For this reason, I would ask all senators to pay close attention to Amendment 21.8. [[Applause from the PD Group and Senators Casini and De Cristofaro.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u46	CALIENDO (FI-PdL XVII). Mr President, I would like to ask for a moment's attention. I think it is necessary to take into account what we are discussing. I hear about presidentialism and I don't. We are discussing the way in which the President of the Republic is elected, no more or less. Talking about presidentialism instead means dealing with the form of government. Mr President, the 1948 Constitution was based on the centrality of Parliament, which was at the heart of all power in our country. The reform we are approving shifts the institutional centre of gravity on the President-in-Office of the Council and there is no alternative. It is not enough for almost 80 MEPs to rebalance this shift in the power centre over the President-in-Office of the Council. By maintaining the President of the Republic as a guarantee power, as desired by the Constituent in 1948, we must strengthen him in his election and it cannot be enough - I apologise to Senator Gotor - to foresee the participation of 80 MEPs, because they do not affect what will be the philosophy of the new state, of the new architrave that comes to be created with a President of the Council that will have what today Zagrebelsky defines as the executive, that is, a possibility of affecting the institutional and constitutional balances. Then I ask myself a question concerning the direct election of the President of the Republic. I am referring to Senator Casini's amendments 21.13 and 21.12, first signed by Senator Gasparri, which do not affect the form of government, so I do not see what the problem of their eligibility is. It is a method of election that strengthens the power of guarantee of the President of the Republic. According to the current forecast, the President of the Republic, in a system that would be decided and governed by the President of the Council, is elected by an unbalanced parliamentary majority, taking into account the fact that, even with the 80 MEPs, we would have no more or less parliamentary majority than the majority in the country. Then it will be what will determine the election of the President of the Republic, which in this way is no longer a guarantee power. I do not see what the concern is: if it does not affect those who are the powers of government and maintain the characteristics of the President of the Republic as a guarantee, why should there not be a rebalancing, with respect to the choice of the President of the Council, with a President of the Republic chosen by the citizens? This is the only way to rebalance, not the addition of 80 more people, who would have no other role than to respond to the secretariats of the parties that elected them to the European Parliament. In the latter case, we would have no more or less further strengthening of the President-in-Office of the Council. Then I believe that a minimum of thought, with calmness, should lead us all to reason. We must, in view of the point we have reached, introduce into the Constitution those counterweights that the Constituents had managed to identify in the President of the Republic, in the Constitutional Court and in the Supreme Council of the judiciary. Otherwise we will have a nice concession from those who will have control of Parliament for the election of the members of the Supreme Council of the judiciary and the Constitutional Court. But at least as far as the President of the Republic is concerned, we guarantee the citizens the possibility of an institutional figure, which will also have to verify the laws before the promulgation, which is free from any conditioning. Mr President, at this time I would be opposed to an election of the President of the Republic which would affect the form of government. That's not how it is, and I don't want to be in your shoes. But I think you would have the great merit of acknowledging that, by not affecting the form of government, there is no doubt about the admissibility of the amendments, because it is a form of election of the President of the Republic to guarantee it, in a system that will see a huge power of the President of the Council, the ability to be that element of mediation and moral suasion which it represented in these 70 years of Republic. I believe that we must have the conscience and freedom, each of us, regardless of the disciplines of party and group, to vote freely on this subject, which represents neither more nor less, the rebalancing from the point of view of the guarantees of all. Mr President, the Grandi Autonomie e Libertà Group will support the first-signed amendment by Senator Gasparri and allow me to support this position with brief and rapid reflections. I will not enter into the purely constitutional question of the balance of powers, but I will enter into the heart of an issue which is certainly an abused concept: popular sovereignty. We have taken on a responsibility, even if it is true that those who have drafted a constitutional reform from fast food are taking it, in which we are moving forward with a majority vote and prevent this House from being able to express itself with the secret vote (as we have seen so far, the only two times that have taken place with a secret vote, the majorities that have a very different dimension have been completely overturned by the obvious vote). We must then decide whether to give the Italian people the opportunity to express themselves with the election in a distinct and distant way from the normal games between the political forces for the formation of the Government, being the President of the Republic not only the notary but also the controller, the guarantor of the perfect observance of the Constitutional Charter or whether to put everything in the cauldron of the political dialectics (living God if it were always a political dialectics), in the game of the do ut des between composition of the Government, Ministers, Undersecretaries and Presidency of the Republic. If we believe, we can refer to this principle, which is the fundamental principle on which the State is founded, which is a convention freely accepted by citizens who are bearers of rights prior to the State itself, a convention that is accepted insofar as everyone feels that he is a creator and participates in the choices that must guide the organization of the State itself, otherwise we will create two external bodies: that of those who have the power to influence the organization and the indication of those who must manage the state power and that of those who indirectly only have to be called to vote in well defined circumstances, in our case (having abolished the elective Senate) in the sole election for the Chamber of Deputies. Let me say that politics is not only the moment when a plan of power is realized: it is a bit like philosophy. They once asked Bertrand Russell to define what philosophy was. He replied that philosophy could be represented with the story of that traveler who, after having lost himself by bicycle in the English countryside, not knowing what the way to Winchester (the city he had to reach) stopped in an isolated house and asked the grocer to point it out to him; The grocer, who was in the back shop, did not hear the question, if he had it repeated several times: in the end, when he heard it, he replied that he did not know what the road was. So, like philosophy, politics is used to ask questions, not to give answers. The question I ask my eminent colleagues here, first of all to Dr. Gromyko, in art Senator Zanda, is what our function is with respect to the people. What are we in here, if not the fruit of the popular will? And why should this popular will not be expressed in a direct and sovereign way by the people to elect the highest office of the Italian State? That is the question I ask, and the legal questions about whether or not this issue is present in the Commission's text are worthless. This is a point of junction: did you want to suppress freedom of expression for the election of senators? Then allow the Italian people to choose the supreme guarantor of this Constitution.

The next stage, which we will also find on our tables, will be to face the electoral law, namely the Italicum, with which even in the other House we will see a direct choice of members by the citizens. The leaders of the parties will instead produce closed and blocked lists, which, according to the election, will produce with a majority prize the new majority of the House, which, in the various subsequent elections of its President, will have the numbers to elect the new President of the Republic. That same majority will be able to elect two thirds of the most important judicial bodies of the constitutional system, so citizens will no longer be called upon to choose any level of government. Pass me the term because it's very strong, but all this knows so much about "lottery": who wins the elections, wins the lottery or a jackpot, so it controls the House, the only one that gives confidence, expresses a government, the government's control bodies and the only guarantee body that should stay out of all the games to ensure and monitor a process that should be democratic. At this point, since we have understood the picture that is emerging, we cannot prevent citizens from electing the only available office, free and guarantor of the whole democratic system in a direct way. This was one of the ten points the League had asked the government for. We now have a series of amendments which provide for direct election, such as that tabled by Senator Gasparri, to a direct election in a sequential phase. Let the election take place after a number of elections with qualified majorities, which cannot be those expressed by the majority prize, or that it is directed, we must give the ball to the citizens. The alternative, ladies and gentlemen, is to replace Article 1 of the Constitution, which in the second paragraph today reads 'Sovereignty belongs to the people', rewriting it as follows: Sovereignty belongs to the leader of the party who is lucky enough to win the next elections. ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u65	Mr Moscardelli (PD). Mr President of the Senate, representatives of the Government, senators and colleagues, the issue of the President of the Republic is fascinating because it is a key element in the weights and counterweights of our constitutional system and therefore in the maintenance of the overall order. However, particularly at this time when the debate focused on the amendment proposed by Senator Casini and which Mr Mineo has decided to support, it seems to me that this type of remedy that is being proposed, i.e. a direct election as a guarantee, is actually incomprehensible and inconsistent precisely with the issue of the guarantee that is being put at the centre. In fact, it is clear that the maximum functionality of the President of the Republic as a guarantee body, as thought by the constituent Fathers, was perfectly combined with the pure proportional system, thus leading to the decision of his election in a condition for the overall sharing of a Parliament in a joint session in integrated composition, with senators, deputies and regional delegates with those numbers, even if this has always led to the expression of a President of the Republic as an expression of the majority (of course very often of great profile, even if not always of the same level). Today, however, with this amendment we are going to introduce the direct election of the President, who is absolutely strident, opposed and inconsistent with the guarantee function. In fact, we could simply find ourselves electing a President who is politically opposed to the majority that won from the ballot box to the general elections. We could surreptitiously introduce a sort of semi-presidency to French, although the President of the French Republic has not only functions of guarantee but also of government. We could, therefore, introduce this dichotomy. You cannot help but glimpse into the eyes of the swindling that we are going to create with a direct election of the President after a few scans gone empty. And we know that, in the face of this kind of possibility, it would be very easy to slip towards the direct election of the President. However, I would like to point out that the same problems we are trying to deal with today are still ahead of us. Since the introduction of the majority system, we have an expression of a majority president, even a small majority. Suffice it to see the case, in 2006, of President Napolitano, who was elected with a political majority reduced to the political elections. If a President should eventually decide to hold a behaviour that is materially contrary to the Constitution, not by formal acts but by conduct that goes against the Constitution, the only system of closure and safeguard is the prosecution, and with directly elected parliamentarians, both with the uninomial colleges Mattarellum, 90% chosen by the secretariats of the parties on safe colleges, or with blocked lists: it is clear that, in this case, we have one of the main points of the problem. On the other hand, if we had Members who had received a direct consent from the citizens and not through blocked lists, this would undoubtedly be a greater guarantee than a President who did not adhere to the constitutional dictation. But to think of solving the problem of a narrowing of the audience with a Senate of 100, in order to guarantee greater value to the function of guarantee body of the President of the Republic, with his direct election, frankly I find it absurd, especially for those who fight against overcoming the perfect bicameralism, because this would attribute who knows what powers to the Executive.

ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u68	LUMIA (PD). Mr President, first of all I would like to ask Senator Gotor to sign his amendment 21.8. Our Constitution has a wonderful system of weights and counterweights, an almost perfect system of balance between the various democratic realities and the same popular representation. But it has two weaknesses, Mr President: democratic decision and democratic participation. The text we are passing through this House, after the work we have done in the Commission, allows us to respond to this crisis by placing the challenge of decision-making and democratic participation in the heart of legislative power, Parliament, which, modified and reformed, is able to respond with a better democratic decision and a more qualified democratic participation. Presidentialism could be a possible way out of the crisis: another system, another system and, therefore, another response to the crisis. These are two different plants: the first is what we are going through and which responds better to the crisis of Italian society; the second is another response, another model. That is why, Mr President, Amendment 21.8 tabled by Senator Gotor and other senators strengthens the system that we have established and that I think is more to be supported than presidentialism in the various versions proposed here, which has another model of installation than our crisis and does not correspond to the evolution of Italian society.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u69	Senator Gotor, do you accept Senator Lumia's signature on Amendment 21.8? Mr Gotor (PD). Yes, Mr. President. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. Ladies and gentlemen, the extensive explanation of the amendments tabled on Articles 21, 22, 24 and 25 has been completed, in the light of which there is no doubt - it has become clear - about the importance of the issues dealt with. However, the Presidency cannot go into the matter, but it must take into account the technical points that have been made. In its opinion on the admissibility of the amendments referred to Articles 21, 22, 24 and 25, which are related and additional, the Presidency drew on the following criteria: firstly, it considers the Commission's decision to combine only a few of the numerous proposals to amend the Constitution of Parliamentary Initiative with the draft law, excluding those specifically addressed by the amendments on the direct election of the Head of State and the form of government; secondly, it considers it necessary to take into account the assessment of inadmissibility already carried out in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, in direct consequence of the previous decision - precisely - to exclude those issues and related draft laws that could be linked. The Presidency cannot escape the fact that the very title of the text approved by the Constitutional Affairs Commission does not make a general reference to changes to Part II of the Constitution, but limits on time the subject to provisions for overcoming the bicameralism of equality, the reduction of the number of parliamentarians, the reduction of the running costs of the institutions, the abolition of the CNEL and the revision of Title V of Part II of the Constitution. For these reasons, the Presidency considers, pursuant to Rule 97 (1) of the Rules of Procedure, that the following amendments should not be considered: 21,2, 21,3, 21,4, 21,5, 21.11, 21.12, 21.13, 22.6, 24.6, 24.7, 24.08, 24.9, 24.10, 24.12, 24.13, 24.0.1, 24.0.2, 24.0.3, 24.0.3, 24.0.4, 24.0.5, 24.0.6, 24.0.7, 24.0.8, 24.0.9, 24.0.11, 24.0.12, 24.0.14, 24.0.16, 24.0.17, 24.0.19, 24.0.20, 24.0.2, 25.1, 25.2, 25.3, 25.4 and 25.10. I invite the rapporteurs and the representative of the Government to comment on these amendments.

ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u71	They have already been examined by the Commission.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u72	FINDING, rapporteur. President Casini's amendments had not been tabled in the Commission, but were tabled in the Chamber, and they contain the principle of election by universal and direct suffrage of the President of the Republic, even in the context of the ballot, and I understand that these too have been declared inadmissible. Therefore, one of the issues on which the rapporteurs would have liked to spread would no longer have reason to be raised. However, I would also like to explain the views of the rapporteurs on Amendment 21.8 tabled by Senator Gotor, which provides for the enlargement of the audience for the election of the President of the Republic to Members of the European Parliament. First of all, I would like to thank Senator Gotor for having contributed once again to our debate. As my colleagues will probably remember - I am sure the Members of the Commission remember this - the current text before the House (which provides for qualified majority voting for the election of the President of the Republic until the ninth vote) is the result of an amendment tabled by Senator Gotor in the Commission, which has been discussed a long time in that Chamber and, finally, by the majority of that vote. This is the text that the rapporteurs are submitting to the House today. The further amendment tabled today by Senator Gotor, signed by many colleagues, certainly attempts to respond to the need for enlargement of the audience. The enlargement of the electorate of the President of the Republic does not arise on the basis of a need, as it were, unknown to the Commission and to our debate. On the contrary, as my fellow Members know, many times it has been thought about the need to have a substantial solution to the problem of the disparity in the consistency of the two Chambers which are called upon to elect the President of the Republic in a joint sitting. A number of solutions have been rejected which have also been put forward in the Commission by other amendments, but also in this House, such as the reduction in the number of Members, who have already voted, or that of the increase in the number of regional delegates, who have been rejected by a large number of colleagues who have spoken in the Commission on the reason why the Senate is already representative of the Regions and, therefore, the origin of the completion of the electoral base for the election of the President of the Republic (which, as you know, under the current Constitution, provides for a certain number of representatives per Region) was already acquitted by the fact that - this time - a Senate would participate in the election which is itself representative of the Regions. Therefore, I do not deny that the need exists; on the contrary, I know that it is very common among colleagues in the various political formations. However, I have a duty to express our doubts as to whether it is the Members of the European Parliament who make up the college. This is for a reason that I do not believe we have thought enough about and that we have worked on in-depth. We would be faced with the unilateral nature of a decision that would all be embedded in the constitutional dynamics within our nation state and that would create interference with the agreements between states. In fact, it is agreements between States that have defined the competences and functions of Members of the European Parliament. And they have defined them on the basis of an assessment of the balance between the needs of the different national states that make up the Union. In this sense, a decision extending the powers of Members of the European Parliament could fully be a violation of the Union's Constitutional Treaties. That is why, on the basis of very sensitive arguments, I would ask Senator Gotor and the many colleagues who have signed his amendment, considering it absolutely well founded - and I hope to be satisfied at the next reading in the House of Members - the need they propose, to withdraw amendment 21.8. This is in order to prevent, of course, all internal reasons for the care we have at this stage on the modernisation of our constitutional system from interfering heavily on an agreement system that concerns our country's membership of the European Union and its institutions.

ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u132	Mr Gotor (PD). Mr President, I owe a reply to Senator Finocchiaro, of whom I know and recognize the authority and, at the same time, the institutional and political sensitivity with which he has addressed this issue over the last few months. With regard to its arguments, I believe that it would not be possible for the European Parliament and the European Union to object - as Senator Mauro also recalled - because the institutions of the Union are committed by the Treaties to respect for the constitutional identity of the Member States. At this moment we are intervening on a source of constitutional rank and therefore I see the possibility of an impediment and supremacy of European law, because the constitutional legislator has no limits, except those established by the fundamental principles of the Constitution and that is the only thing that should be measured. Finally, there is the possibility of interpreting the new trend, which is all political, to reintegrate relations and relations between European Parliaments and national systems. In a recent book by two constitutionalists like Manzella and Lupo, we are talking about opening a third life in the European Parliament because - look - an excess of sovereignty has been and is the real reason that, in my opinion, has alienated and is driving Europe away from its citizens. There is also, of course, beyond the reasons for the legal support of an amendment, a political crux, and that is what I would like to draw the House's attention to. The political issue we have discussed so many times in the Commission is that if the current reform has a weakness, it is precisely the issue of the guarantees and balances of the whole system: President Quagliariello reminded us yesterday with his usual lucidity. And what better guarantee - these seventy years of republican history teach us - than that coming from a President of the Republic will and guarantor? I believe that we must do everything to keep this function unchanged: I repeat that it would be a serious mistake - and this is the only reason why I am unable to withdraw this amendment - to believe that only the protection and guarantee of the secret vote or the strengthening of quorums are sufficient to contain the possibility of the break-in of a new force in the system, which takes the majority prize, even for one vote, and then with extreme ease - a facility that we have the civil and political duty to make complicated or more complicated - let me take the popular expression - "all the cucuzzaro". With this amendment, in conclusion, there is a technical and practical measure: it spreads chronologically over three different moments - the political, regional and European elections - the necessary consensus so that the guarantees of constitutional balances can be broken. For this reason, I continue to entrust the fate of this amendment to the hands, minds and conscience of all Members and all the senators of this House (and it costs me both to say it and to have to do it): in the choice we will make in a few moments, it will live the ability to further enhance our autonomy as senators in the context of this constitutional reform. I believe that the Government - which we support and strongly support - could also strengthen itself before the public in a choice in favour of guarantees and respect for the Constitution, when we are changing it.

ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u144	Sunta (SPI). Mr President, I would like to say that I am also speaking on behalf of my colleagues Mr Lanzillotta and Mr Maran. We are aware of the delicate passage that we are experiencing in the reform. So, before I declare the vote on the proposal, I would simply like to make a few comments on the reason why we have signed the amendment in question. I have no claim to refute the reasoning put forward by President Finocchiaro. As a simple legal operator and also as a person who has served as a Member of the European Parliament for several years, I would, however, like to observe the following. In the development of the European Union, we have seen in recent years a progressive transfer of sovereignty, which in about thirty years has seen the European Parliament move from a governing body and political representation to becoming a fully co-legislator with the Council in relation to the matters on which sovereignty has been transferred. This has changed the face of European legislation, and today we have a distinction between regulations and directives: the one, to which we citizens must stick in individual states, are immediately effective in legislation; the other, however, still require Parliament's mediation and transposition within the framework designed by the Council and the European Parliament. In recent years, since 1957, we have seen the extension of the powers of other bodies emanating from the European Union, which find their foundation in the institutions, including the democratic institutions of the European Union. I am thinking, for example, of the Court of Justice, to which citizens can immediately turn for matters on which there has been the transfer of sovereignty and whose judgments are immediately effective. How can we argue that decisions taken, for example by regulations or judgments themselves, which are born of a body that has issued that legislative process, do not have immediate and direct effect on our lives? For this reason, it would be important for Members of the European Parliament to be called, as the regional councillors were called, to elect the President of the Republic, because the democratic legitimacy they have today leads them to contribute to legislation which is immediately effective even at national level. We realize that a reform is also the result of political mediation and that today there are conditions that did not exist years ago, when other attempts at reform failed. That is precisely why we have withdrawn Amendment No 21.6 and have tabled an agenda, which calls on the Government to make a commitment to resolve this issue in the House of Deputies. For this reason, I no longer ask the rapporteurs to find a mediation, but I appeal to the Government to ensure that, at the second reading of the provision in the Chamber of Deputies, it becomes a guarantor of the heartfelt and strong motion within the Senate to widen the electoral audience of the President of the Republic, in order to prevent a single party from somehow electing the Head of State on its own, at the ninth vote or after the ninth vote. These are delicate passages on which we must certainly have the capacity of political mediation, but we must also take care of the problem itself. I therefore believe that it is right - not because we have proposed it, but because it is a way of affirming the principle - to record a problem felt and to ask the Government to assume responsibility so that, in view of the second reading of the measure, the issue relating to the audience of the election of the President of the Republic is taken care of. After the outcome of the response on the agenda, we will consider what to do with this amendment.

I think that this is the greatest guarantee of the quality of our work and I think that today we would do very well to approve the Commission text with the nine qualified votes, knowing that those nine qualified majority votes and the secret vote that the Constitution provides for the election of the President of the Republic are the highest guarantees that we have so far found. ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u162	FERRARA Mario (GAL). And no, Mr President, I asked not to have a short time. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. Doesn't it have a short time? I'll give you two minutes. FERRARA Mario (GAL). If you allow me, also because I haven't spoken for a few days and I promise not to speak anymore, but I believe that the debate that is taking place and the explanations of vote are a little final summary of the work we have done. I insist that I disagree with Senator D'Anna for once because Senator Zanda doesn't look like Gromyko. Among other things, he does not share with Gromyko what he shared with Breznev and Podgornyj: they were all three chemical engineers and of the same course. President Zanda is instead a jurist, which is not me who, alas, have the same defect as Gromyko, Podgornyj and Breznev: I am an engineer. And in order to be able to listen carefully I had to study, study a lot and read some books, even those of Quagliariello. There I have learned a few things that I will try to say, namely that the main reason for the debate is that those who speak and those who have put forward their proposals believe that what we are hesitating about and who have been hesitated by the Commission is an unbalanced text, even if it is unorganized. In fact, in the debate, as in the period from 1945 to 1947, there was a choice to be made, namely whether to proceed on the path of presidentialism or semi-presidency or on that of parliamentary democracy as in modern times is interpreted, namely a parliamentary democracy with Chancellor or strong chancellor. In 1945-1947 there was so much shame in talking about the Westminster model - which was done, as Quagliariello remembers in his books, in 1958 and 1962 in France when the two Constitutions I will talk about shortly - that instead of talking about this model we began to talk, as far as parliamentary democracy is concerned, about the Norwegian model. Both Orlando and Croce, who all studied the Norwegian model, both quoted him and discovered that it was the same thing as the English model, the Westminster model. We could not then talk about the presidential or semi-presidency model, that is to say the French model that did not exist, since the presidential system that they could take for example was only the American one, which, however, had in the figure of the President a danger that was the drift that had already occurred in the previous decades in Europe, both in Germany and in Italy. They then chose the model of parliamentary democracy and discussed whether that parliamentary democracy could become a chancellor, but there were no examples to the world of chancellor other than the English one, the Westminster model. In that concern, they decided not to give the Premier any power. From that system, except for the first ten years, from 1948 to 1958, from the De Gasperi Governments, characterized by stability, with a great transport and labor in the country, but also with a consensus that derived from the post-war situation, it has derived, from 1958 until today, that instability that in the past years, in 2006, in the bicameral Commission of Alema and still today in the speeches we are making, has put the need for change. On this need for change it has been decided in a way that intervenes in a parliamentary type of democracy to chancellorate, which is the one hesitated by the 1st Commission. But what is the flaw? What is the flaw, which is why we then have the Gasparri amendment, the Casini amendment, the Gotor amendment and the Senator Chiti who speaks? The problem is that in every chancellor, the Chancellor has the power to dissolve Parliament, but this is not provided for in this proposal for a constitutional change, and then, surreptitiously, he is given a power that is not to dissolve Parliament, but to constitute it. That is why we are not talking about electoral law, we are not talking about mutualizing a foundation in the Italian Constitution, which is why the English Constitution works, namely Article 38 of the English Constitution that constitutionalizes the electoral law. Another very important issue beyond 38 is 1937, the date of approval of the Irish Constitution, in which, since there is no great tradition of winning-takes-all in Ireland that exists in America and England, they constitutionalized the electoral law. That is why this reform of the Constitution cannot work. And Senator Quagliariello is well aware, who at this point invokes, with respect to the French Constitution of 1958 (he knows it well, because he also wrote it in the documents of the sages), the Constitution of 1962. When the people see that this constitutional change will not work, then it will happen as in France, when, compared to the Premier's statement made by Parliament, there came to an amendment, with direct election and semi-presidentialism (which was in fact introduced in France in 1958, but in 1962). Senator Quagliariello knows that and others know it well. But, instead of playing with truth, they play flowery. And so at this point the amendment, the non-eligibility, the forcing, the outcome of the Commission for a Constitution that cannot work. ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u169	Mr President, such a broad debate and so rich in issues obviously cannot see the rapporteurs and certainly cannot see me silent in the face of the many objections that have come from my fellow Members. I would like to make a very small and very brief premise; I refer to Senator Chiti and Senator Bocchino. I thank everyone for their confidence in my role as rapporteur, and I think I can also speak on behalf of Senator Calderoli. However, we are rapporteurs, not dominating the measure, and the first task of the rapporteurs is to gather the sense of the debate, recording the possible majorities that appear on the different points of the reform and those proposed to the House. That is what a rapporteur must do. If Anna Finocchiaro or Roberto Calderoli were to write their reform, it would probably be quite different from the text under discussion today. [[Applause from the PD Group]] We are the rapporteurs for the measure, guaranteeing that what is proposed to the House, unless it is denied or rethought by the Assembly, collects what is considered to be the most shared thought, always in the logic that the law is, precisely, transaction. It is the House that dominates this issue. The rapporteurs can and must confine themselves to giving guidance, providing arguments and motivating the reasons for their choices. In this sense, I believe that there is some basis for Senator Gasparri's gracious revelation, which he said: President Finocchiaro didn't answer me. I did not do this solely because Senator Gasparri's amendment, like all the others who alluded, as Senator Gasparri says, exclusively to another way of electing the President of the Republic, had been declared inadmissible. At this point, however, I would like to put the following question to the House, although it is no longer on the agenda and there are no amendments to vote on. In an intact constitutional system, because the form of government is not reviewed, the direct election of the President of the Republic means that this President of the Republic, who is the son of a majority and who has the political weight deriving from a direct investiture, can dissolve the Chambers, presides over the CSM, appoints the judges of the Constitutional Court, is the head of the Armed Forces. I believe that this is to dispel any doubt that we cannot change by using the universal and direct vote the way we elect the President of the Republic without, of course, looking at the need to balance the system. The only attempt that was made in this sense, which was an attempt entirely based on the personality of De Gaulle, as history teaches us, was overwhelmed by the experience of the Fifth Republic. President Chiti, but also Senator Maran, in some way reduced the participation of MEPs in the election of the President of the Republic on two issues. Senator Maran asks why a Member of the European Parliament could not take part in an Italian Commission (I imagine he refers to a committee of government advice or, in any case, to another constitutional body) and President Chiti says that Parliament in common sitting is nevertheless a way in which Parliament meets and is not a body. Why not? Parliament in common sitting is a constitutional body which may also have its own rules but which has up to now adopted the Rules of Procedure of the House, so it is a constitutional body. We would therefore establish that Members of the European Parliament, who do not represent Italian citizens, but represent by treaty the citizens of the European Union, so much so that they may not also be of Italian citizenship, being candidates and elected in Italy, would come to exercise functions in a constitutional body such as Parliament in common sitting. I do not want to go further, it is possible that I am wrong, colleagues (of course Anna Finocchiaro is not wrong, who is not at all an expert on these issues, as I do not think so many are in this House), but of course we have documented ourselves before giving an opinion, as it is the duty of anyone to give an opinion on such a challenging issue. I would just like to point out that according to part of the doctrine - which I do not share, but that is not the point - the agendas for the European Parliament's meetings are also practical and, where participation in the European Parliament's committees and meetings is prevented by the fact that those Members of the European Parliament make up a constitutional body at the time, there would certainly be a profile of interference with the wills of pacts defined in the Treaties and with the Treaties which define the competences, functions and tasks of the Members of the European Parliament. Finally, to reply to one final objection, that of those who claim that no progress has been made, I would like to remind you that the original text of the Government's draft law provided that the modalities of election of the President of the Republic would remain unchanged, therefore the two-thirds majority for the first three ballots and then the absolute majority. Today we have a text before the House - and this was done in the adoption of an amendment by Senator Gotor - which provides that we shall vote by a two-thirds majority for the first four votes, by a three-fifths majority for the remaining four votes and by an absolute majority only by the ninth vote. It is therefore not true that the Commission's evaluation work has been either yielding or giving up on what was proposed by the Government's bill. That this is not enough seems to me to be an assessment that has come from many. In the circumstances given, I believe that the rapporteurs have done well to defend this text. [[Applause from the PD and NCD groups]]

ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u247	MAURO Giovanni (GAL). Mr President, I will only steal a few minutes from the House. 26.0.1 is one of the four amendments I tabled together with Senator Tremonti, which concern the inclusion in the Constitution of so-called clauses respecting European constraints. I would like to remind the House that we are the only European country to have included in the Constitution the decrease in public debt. All other European countries have included in their Constitutions the principle of not increasing public debt; instead, we are the only ones committed to reducing public debt, which is really castrating, is a noose that we have around our necks. I remember, among other things, that the Berlusconi Government prepared the constitutional rule for the maintenance of limits that did not include the fiscal compact, which was then introduced with the Monti Government in 2012. With this amendment and with the other subsequent amendments that we have tabled, we want to support the implementation of this Government's programme. If it is true, as it is true, that the Renzi Government intends to carry out the battle so that investment expenditure does not form part of the calculation of the deficit-GDP ratio and if it is true, as it is true, that we intend to relaunch our economy also through a different relationship with the instruments and bodies of the European Community, it is also true that we must equip the Government with an instrument that will enable it to do so. I therefore call on the House and also the Government - I do not think the Commission can give its own indication on these amendments, but it can return to the House - to consider these as amendments of absolute reasonableness, which have also been checked from a technical point of view, so that they can be approved without violating any of the Treaties and relations with the European Union. We intend to bring Italy back to the same standards of commitment as other European partners. I therefore call on the House to vote in favour of this amendment and the following three that I have mentioned, which are based on the same ratio and philosophy.

It seems almost absurd to have to stress something that should already be in the state of affairs, since it is a basic principle in relations, but in reality this is not the case when we have a government administration and bureaucracy and, above all, taxation that weigh heavily and stifle families and businesses. ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u255	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, this morning on the Corriere della Sera (Corriere della Sera), Carlo Nordio, the magistrate who was best acquainted with the legal aberrations of bribes in his book "Justice" published in 1997 by Guerini, gave his support to a proposal that was in vain made in recent years. I am referring to the election by drawing lots of members of the Supreme Council of the judiciary. It is, as one Minister of Justice, Mr Alfano, said, a proposal that cuts off any possible current chain. As is well known, there is virtually no electoral system capable of preventing the three or four major Italian judicial districts from infusing the CSM and consequently managing disciplinary systems and promotions in a clientele way. President Cossiga's ancient polemic against the judiciary, order and not power, does not require re-enactment here. Here we simply say that the Supreme Council of the judiciary is not a third Chamber, it does not express any popular sovereignty, but it is a technical-professional seat of self-government of the judiciary. Whether it is order or power, the judiciary certainly expresses a level of professionalism that does not justify any skepticism about the effects of the draw among its members. If in the face of tens of thousands of actions of responsibility against doctors in 20 years there are only four against the magistrates (data of the "Garantista" this morning), then we certainly do not run the risk that they will be drawn for the CSM magistrates less than excellent. The Italian Socialist Party proposed on the point draft law No 1547, amendments to Article 104. Amendment 27.0.6 achieves the same objective, even if it refers to the ordinary law the subsequent determination of the application modalities of the stated principle. It will also apply to lay members as part of the lists drawn up by Parliament, in accordance with the existing system for aggregated members of the Constitutional Court. Ladies and gentlemen, if we want to steal the judiciary from the current drift against which this House has gone on several occasions, this is the absolute surest way. Beyond the constraints of deployment and agreement, I invite you to approve it.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u256	Mr President, Amendment 27.1 proposes to delete the article because I would like to make a comment.
