Secondly, we must propose to replace this function or this function, if it is considered non-existent, with institutions that perform similar functions. Among other things, the repeal of Article 99 of the Constitution implies that we must refer in any case to all the legislation that the CNEL referred to, and in this regard I wonder if we do not create a whole series of coordination problems that, as we move forward in the discussion of the draft law, seem to be increasingly thick and relevant, as is increasingly highlighted in the current debate. It is a bit in the history of this country that we proceed to the creation of agencies, committees and councils, and then, when we verify the non-functioning, to their suppression, going to create something new and different or reproducing them in the same way: I think it is a typical Italian misconception. Perhaps, however, at a time when we are dealing with a relevant text such as the Constitution, it would be more common sense and more correct to try to get on with the substance, without proceeding in this rapid way. ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u266	Parent (PD). Mr President, my Group will vote in favour of repealing Article 99 of the Constitution, but I will spend a few minutes talking about the CNEL. The Constitutions assigned the CNEL two essential tasks, in addition of course to the function of legislative initiative: the first was to compose the interests that characterize the dialectic between the different economic and social categories, and the second task was very well explained by the first President of the CNEL, Meuccio Ruini, who said: The CNEL has an intermediate function; it does not reach the decision (...); but its function is not merely a study; it is a preparation; it is like a bridge between two moments of examination and action. The CNEL in recent years has tried to play the constitutional role entrusted to it. I remember only a few of the initiatives and tasks carried out: he managed the national archive of collective labour agreements; he contributed to the identification of quality of life indicators, which have also entered this year's DEF; he carried out an annual report on the labour market, an immigration report and support for territorial pacts. Among the last legislative initiatives I mention two: the one on the budget and the other on gender statistics. But the country's decision-making system over the years has not harnessed this potential. This serious lack, unfortunately structural, does not depend on the type of organization or who governs it, but on a culture and a working method. In Italy - perhaps Senator Mussini mentioned this too - no institutional bodies have been used to identify technical solutions with a scientific basis, above the parties, often depriving Parliament of objective instruments to support legislative development. This way of proceeding has progressively made CNEL lose its role, a role envisaged by the Constituents, making the structure not an opportunity but a cost. Of course, if in more than fifty years we have degraded the CNEL's work and failed to make sense of it, it is clear that we need to find another method. This means going beyond the present organism. Let's make a recommendation to the government. The G27.4 agenda has now been adopted, but with the abolition of the CNEL we will have to find and think of places and moments when we can express the social dialogue that is also the foundation of the European Union, also because Italy must ensure its participation both in the European Economic and Social Committee and in the International Association of Economic and Social Councils.

ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u283	DIVINE (LN-Aut). Mr President, I assume that my Group will vote in favour of Rule 27, but there are still doubts. Article 99 of the Constitution consists of three paragraphs; there is no doubt that, having already decided on the part of the legislative initiative, there is no question about the repeal of the third paragraph, namely that the CNEL will no longer have the power of legislative initiative. Nor are there any problems with paragraph 2, as the Government or the Chambers can decide by what type of technical and economic advice they can be assisted. As for paragraph 1, we can also write that the CNEL is abolished, but this does not have any effect: the structure and the palace remain, the staff remains such and what. Therefore, rules will be needed to determine what that staff will do, where the sector will end up, what the structure will do. On two points there is no objection; on the contrary, we have long thought that organs that have manifested inability and inefficiency, and that in any case have proved inadequate to the task for which they had been thought, surely can be set aside. However, it would require a reference to a rule that would determine exactly what professionalism and experience will do. I am not referring to the elected, who will no longer be expected, but to the whole apparatus of the CNEL. To conclude, there will be no more election, we will no longer have economic categories participating in that body. Fedeli, Santini and the others who have subscribed to Amendments 27.3 and 27.4 should also have maintained this point, because we cannot say that ours is a system with wide and widespread participation, after which we no longer question the economic categories, which we are interested only when there is to be offset by increasing taxation. If they count for something in this system, they must also have their own political weight, which in the Constitution must be somehow guaranteed.

In short, rivers of rules have been squandered on the Provinces, which have made this issue increasingly chaotic. We need to understand how the hierarchy of entities will be articulated, or in any case the whole of the entities that form the Republic (State, Regions, Provinces, Municipalities, metropolitan entities). By deleting the Provinces, we have started a very chaotic path, with the provision of Article 10, which provides what the Regions will be, or rather, what their competences will be, if they will be legislative, and in what sphere they will be able to act. From the original text of the Government, the Regions disappeared as bodies with normative competences, but remained as essentially administrative bodies. If we delete the Provinces, they remain the Municipalities, which are essentially administrative bodies, but we no longer have a dialectic and a distribution of powers between the State and the Provinces. I could say: What do I care? I come from an autonomous Province, with a guaranteed Statute, which is constitutional law, and the competences of the special autonomous and autonomous Provinces will not touch each other, but that is not the point. What happens to the Regions? We have put aside the problem, but today we are going to cancel the Provinces and we do not know what the Regions will do, if they will be substantially maxiprovinces without power, because the Government has recalled to itself all the competences of a competing nature. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. Conclude, Divine Senator. DIVINE (LN-Aut). You understand, Mr President, that we are not talking about goat wool. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. I get it. It is a great confusion, this, both in operating and in the overall framework that will emerge.

I'll read you two of his articles to make you understand what we're violating. Article 2 says: "The principle of local autonomy must be recognised by domestic legislation, and, as far as possible, by the Constitution" and so far we are, and then continues: "Local autonomy means the right and effective capacity for local communities to regulate and administer under the law, under their responsibility, and for the benefit of the people, an important part of public affairs." Article 3, paragraph 2, states: "This right is exercised by Councils and Assemblies consisting of members elected by free, secret, equal, direct and universal suffrage, able to have executive bodies responsible for them. This is the European Charter of Local Self-Government which the Italian State has signed. Therefore, I repeat Provinces and metropolitan cities are local self-governments which should be made up of elected members by free, secret, equal, direct and universal suffrage. Secondly, it is also clarified that this practice of differentiated autonomy of the regions presupposes two requirements: balance of budget, i.e. respect for standard costs, demonstrating that it is a virtuous, responsible and non-spending autonomy and respect for the essential levels of performance. ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u356	I would like to start with Article 29, and I would like to refer to Article 30 later. The text approved in the Commission differs from the text currently in force on one aspect, as my colleague, Mr Santini has already pointed out: it is expected that in order to be able to confer more legislative powers, a particular procedure is needed and certain requirements, such as Article 119 of the Constitution, are met. The only condition of merit that places the Region in a position of balance between revenue and expenditure is. I personally consider this to be an extremely dangerous requirement. That is why, with amendment 29.30, signed by other colleagues in the Health Commission, we call for this criterion to be repealed, for a very simple reason; I do not want to waste the time of this House; I understand that we are all in great difficulty after such a long marathon, but it is not conceivable that the allocation of additional powers to the regions, as well as the procedures set out in the article, should be made conditional on the fact that they have a balanced budget. Here too, I am expressing myself in a non-technical way, but understandable to all those who know what is happening in territorial realities. The balanced budget can be obtained by avoiding offering the services that the Regions or municipalities are required to provide to citizens. It has already happened in recent years and those with experience in the health sector know very well that in many cases the budgetary balance has been achieved not by improving the efficiency of the functioning of the system, but simply by reducing the services guaranteed to citizens. Mr Santini rightly added that clarification, namely that the budgets are in balance, but that the essential levels of all benefits relating to civil and social rights are also guaranteed. If this clarification were accepted, it would, in my view, be sufficient. Otherwise, if your amendment were not accepted with that further clarification, I would retain the amendment that provides for the abolition of that aspect. I am speaking on Article 117 of the Constitution. Unfortunately, we have reached the end of this long marathon, but Title V is one of the most complicated and delicate parts and above all - I say this to all of us, to me first - it is perhaps one of the parts that citizens understand better than others, because it concerns the ways in which subnational authorities provide services to citizens, competences, resources available and responsibilities. I wouldn't want us to pay less attention to Title V when we're tired. I would like to make a few comments on some of the amendments which have also been tabled by others for some of them. I refer in particular to Amendments Nos 30,46 and 30,167 for Article 117. I begin with Amendment No 30.46, which I think is the most important. In this case two amendments are proposed: the simplest to illustrate is the one concerning the inclusion of social policies within policies, the general framework of which must be reserved exclusively for the State. We know, in fact, that in the different areas of welfare, social policies are the most unequal between the Municipalities and between the Regions of this country and lack an overall framework, to which everyone must adhere. I do not go any further into this matter (maybe someone can do it better than me later), but it is important and I ask the Government if it is willing to accept this aspect. I know very well that regional administrations do not want to lose their powers in this area but we respond to citizens and not to regional administrations and citizens want services to be guaranteed throughout Italy and not only in regions that are sensitive to this problem. [[Applause from the Mixed-SEL Group and Senator Gatti]] The second aspect of amendment 30.46 concerns the configuration of the type of rules which remain the exclusive competence of the State. In the current wording of the Constitution it is written "General Rules" as amended during the discussion in the Commission in "General and common provisions." I am not a jurist, but all the lawyers, including those who make their important contribution in this Parliament, have pointed out that there are two cases, that we know what they mean and that we can think of overcoming the uncertainties, confusion and overlap currently present in the field of competing legislation. The two are the general rules and the basic principles. [[Brusio. Call the president back.]] I understand why this has been done, but unfortunately a third case has been introduced in the Commission, concerning general and common provisions. We do not know in detail what this case means, so we are overcoming the competing matter, with regard to which we had uncertainties in the application that created, among other things, a certain type of litigation, overlaps and discretionary interpretations, not making clarity but creating a new problem, to introduce general and common provisions that will require interpretative interventions and will risk in any case need of further interventions, probably also of the Constitutional Court, therefore we do not solve the problem for which the current Title V has been criticized and against which we are called to intervene. I therefore call for this matter to be reviewed very carefully, because one problem cannot be solved by proposing another, which creates uncertainty and overlap. The solution is macro-regions. We set up regions with a minimum population of 5 million inhabitants, as my colleague Barani said, perhaps not merging Piedmont and Liguria, but joining Savona and Imperia with Piedmont, La Spezia with Tuscany and Genoa with a Lombard-Piedmont area. The important thing is that they are more or less equal regions, express the same number of senators and have the same representativeness. This solves economic problems: at this moment Liguria weighs on Italy for over 600 million euros, more than it could cost: the 2,700 euros per capita that costs against the 2,200 of Veneto and Lombardy, 500 euros per 1.5 million inhabitants are almost 700 million euros. That's the country's disaster. The economy doesn't really work on small regions. For this reason, in Article 29, I propose the establishment of macro-regions with a minimum population of 5 million inhabitants. As an alternative, in Article 30, I propose the creation of macro-regions of health care: try to think, as far as Liguria is concerned, what Imperia has to do with La Spezia. We think of the territories for health, always territories with at least 5 million inhabitants; the regions below 5 million inhabitants must be annexed for the health part to larger regions: La Spezia will end with Tuscany, Imperia and Savona with Piedmont. You can't have heart surgery just 30 kilometers apart, just because they're on the border. The health plan must not be done at regional level, but at territorial level. I therefore really urge you to think about Article 29 and the constitution of macro-regions or, alternatively, the constitution of territories and macro-regions of health.

ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u372	RICHIUTI (PD). Mr President, I am speaking on Rule 30. One of the main reasons for this reform is to try to reduce the litigation between the State and the Regions before the Constitutional Court. In Article 117 currently in force, in fact, there are exclusive legislative powers of the Regions, of the State and competing competences. The competing competence - which we had copied from Germany in 2001 - makes sense in German cooperative federalism. In our conflicting and somewhat sloppy regionalism, which we have experienced in the last 15 years, the concurrent legislative competence has proved to be the ground for continuous clashes, sometimes even presumptuous. Today we repeal competing competence and I think it is a right choice. It has been shown that the distribution by subject is abstractly the clearest and safest, but in practice it proves to be fallacious. From the dispute before the Constitutional Court we can see that there are some issues that are cross-cutting: the environment, for example, is a matter in part state and partly regional because urban planning has an impact on the ecosystem, referred to in point (s) currently in force. I do not know if we will solve the problem of litigation, but I want to address another profile: in 2003 the Constitutional Court issued a fundamental judgment, No 303, which tried to say that in the exercise of all legislative powers, both exclusive and competing, it was necessary to observe the criterion of loyal cooperation. In essence, it was said that, in order to function, the distribution must be able to count on concrete, collaborative and constructive attitudes of the various players in legislation and public policies. It must therefore be understood that the distribution, both by subject matter and by interests at stake, is to be understood. In that judgment it was said, in fact, that limiting the unifying activity of the State to matters expressly attributed to it in exclusive power or to the determination of the principles in matters of concurrent power, as the Regions argued, would mean but surrounding the legislative competences of the Regions of iron guarantees, but it would also mean devalue too much unitary instances, which even in constitutional structures strongly pervaded by institutional pluralism justify, under certain conditions, a derogation to the normal distribution of competences. The Constitutional Court - and I quote, for example, the judgment n. 14 of 2004 - has also emphasized that Article 117 unifies the State's economic policy instruments that pertain to the development of the entire country: instruments that ultimately express a unitary character and, interpreted by means of each other, are all aimed at balancing the volume of financial resources included in the economic circuit. State intervention is therefore justified by its macro-economic importance. In short, Article 117 is a complicated provision, in which we need to be as clear as possible. Amendment 30.46, which has as its first signatory Senator Dirindin, aims to clarify that it is up to the State, precisely in view of what was stated in judgment No 14 of 2004, to determine social policies, distinct from the essential levels of assistance (LEA). We know that unfortunately a general law on LEAs in Italy has not yet been enacted: we are firm to a decree of the President of the Council of 2001. I believe, however, that it is necessary to make it clear that Article 3 (2) of the Constitution, which requires the Republic to remove the obstacles that limit the freedoms of citizens, should also require us to determine social policies in a progressive sense and to increase levels of social protection. [[Applause from the PD Group.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u401	ICHINO (SPI). Mr President, I should like to explain briefly Amendments 30.43 and 30.45. The first of the two refers to points (g) and (m) of Article 117 of the Constitution, where, with regard to the discipline of contractual employment relationships, the legal discipline of employment is explicitly and extensively indicated in relation to public administrations, while in relation to private employment relations it is only referred to the protection of health and safety at work. This can give rise to an interpretation which is certainly contrary to what everyone in this House wants, namely a division of powers which sees legislative intervention entirely reserved for the State, including on the subject of regulating employment relations between individuals. Amendment 30.43 aims to avoid this possible distortion, which is determined by the fact that only the discipline of employment relationships in the hands of public administrations is referred to as a matter of state competence. Amendment 30.45 instead aims to allow the State, in the field of employment services, to intervene to establish standards and to monitor their compliance by the regions. The amendment is substantially overlapping with part of Senator Dirindin's amendment 30.46 and others and part of Senator Guerra's amendment 30.47 and others. They pose a problem which I think deserves attention from the House.

I remember that the Special Statute, as it had been outlined at the end of the war, had to give room for recovery to the regions lagging behind. Today we find ourselves, instead, in the condition of having to give the Regions that manage to tow the rest of the train the opportunity to be able to do it with full force. We therefore propose specifically, with this amendment, to attribute also to the Lombardy Region the special Statute, in addition to the ordinary one that already has today. This is not an exquisite proposal for an opportunity, but to consider that at the same time as we are voting on this reform, the Chamber of Deputies is closing its work on the competitiveness decree-law, which has been approved in this House, has been sent to the House and will now return to the Senate again. ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u610	FUCKSIA (M5S). Mr President, Amendment 30.41 concerns administrative simplification, which means that we are clearer and better understood, so that everything is easier and more understandable, making the operation of the administration even more logical. Simplifying administrative action means cutting down unnecessary procedural steps, controls and formalities or eliminating anything that is superfluous or even harmful to the proper functioning of the administration. Administrative simplification is a means of improving the relationship with the administration of citizens, economic actors, social formations, and of course all those working within the administrative system itself. In this sense, administrative simplification can be considered synonymous with administrative reform, i.e. an overall change in the administration aimed at making its action more efficient, rapid and economical. The purpose of the above premise is to highlight the importance of an exclusive competence of the State in this matter, in order to allow and ensure the consistent use of simplification tools throughout the national territory, with consequent benefit for all Italian citizens. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. I invite you to conclude, Senator. FUCKSIA (M5S). It is also considered that, for the sake of clarity, administrative simplification applies to all the public administration activities of which the procedure is, however important, a subset. In our view, it is therefore appropriate in the text to clarify the wording proposed by this amendment. [[Applause from the M5S Group.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u611	I'm asking to speak for a statement...

ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u690	FINDING, rapporteur. I want to speak because this debate has also raised so many issues and I would like each of you to know what you are voting for in dealing with the amendments that are now before us. First of all, I would like to reassure my fellow Members that we have started precisely from the need to prevent the conflict between the State and the regions which has occurred so far on particularly sensitive issues such as health and education. As my colleagues point out, in the current Constitution, the subjects of health and education protection are subject to concurrent legislation. That is why in dealing with this issue we have considered that we reserve the exclusive competence of the State to determine the essential levels of benefits relating to civil and social rights which must be guaranteed throughout the national territory. We have also said that it belongs to the exclusive competence of the State to issue general and common provisions; common in the sense that they ensure uniformity on national territory, for the protection of health, for food safety and for the protection and safety of work. In the meantime, constitutional jurisprudence in cases of conflict has explicitly told us two things, precisely with health judgments. First of all, he told us that using the term "general standards" actually conferred on the State a pervasive legislative competence also in the areas of the territorial organisation of health care. The second thing that the Constitutional Court has told us is that undoubtedly belong to the regions the planning and organization of health and social services that, in fact, in the text that is before the Chamber, are entrusted to the competence of the Regions, having disappeared the concurrent legislation. Colleagues, I say this precisely in order to prevent any misunderstanding in this House on a subject on which we all have a particular sensitivity. In this sense, Senator Dirindin's amendment merely reiterates what is already written in Article 117 (m) by replying it identically, with a single amendment, in addition to dividing it into letter m) and m-bis, which re-introduces the term "general standards"; an expression which, as we know, was itself the origin of conflict which then the Constitutional Court had to intervene every time to settle. In this sense, I would ask Senator Dirindin to withdraw her amendment. [[Comments from the M5S Group]] We would welcome the agenda presented by Senator De Biasi, Senator Bianconi and Senator Romano, which, among other things, is to reaffirm the particular care with which the Senate has written and defended this text, which is only at first reading. [[Applause from the PD and NCD groups and Senator Romani Paolo.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2014-08-06-LEG17-Senato-sed-301.ana.u759	PALERMO (Aut (SVP, UV, PATT, UPT)-PSI-MAIE). Mr President, I would like briefly to point out the risk we are facing on this issue. The question of general and common provisions has been raised. From this point of view we want to make a constructive proposal here. We know that the reform aims to reduce conflicts. Today there is the category of general rules, and a new one is introduced, that of general and common provisions. The proposal is to make use of a consolidated category in many systems, that is, of general principles. Very clear. If, among other things, we introduce the term "dispositions," which is more specific than "norms," we certainly extend the links of state intervention. The consequence is that this new category will be defined in its specific content by the Court, necessarily increasing conflicts. In very few cases, law is an exact science, almost never, but I am told that in this case the consequences are largely predictable, they are certain. At least we know what we are going to vote for: more conflicts. We can do that, but we need to know.

The problem is greater if the programming is made - alas - aseptically at the central level of the State, because - let me be honest - you do not have the meter and the thermometer of how the economy takes place at the moment. From personal experience this worries us very much. With these amendments we want to facilitate this situation.

I simply repeat that judgment No 147 of 2012 considered constitutionally unlawful the reduction of the school network because it essentially operated on the sizing of schools and the current text of the Constitution has general rules. Therefore, if we leave the current text, a well-established constitutional jurisprudence will remove any ambiguity regarding the division of tasks with regard to education. On the other hand, if we change into general and common provisions, as the text provides for at the moment, we will generate new disputes that will have major repercussions and will cause great inconvenience not only to education, but also to the government of the territory as the subject of this amendment. Madam President, I am sorry to contradict Senator Uras, a person I particularly value, but I would like to put in line some issues first of all to show that this amendment has no legislative scope and if it had it it would be absolutely subversive of the whole system. The regional statutes do not have competence in relation to the representative Assemblies. And this is a first condition. The amendment then refers to the Parliament and therefore the regional statute would also stand in relation to the election of the Chamber of Deputies. However, this is not the first time that we have voted in this House for an amendment, which is not formal, which is totally untenable from a logical, legal and constitutional point of view. The amendment that we have just voted on in secret was, first of all, a part that had already been covered by the vote of the House; then, with this amendment it is said that it is the regions that have exclusive competence in the matter of national electoral law with which the House of Deputies is composed for the protection of the representations of Albanian, Catalan, Germanic linguistic minorities and so on. Therefore, those who in this case call for compliance with the Constitution as a criterion for dealing with this debate can no longer insist - let me say this - on amendments which are not legal in scope, absolutely contrary to the principles of the legal system, illogical and erroneous is to say little.
